## Statistical Integrity Checker

### efigure1.png

---
**PLOT TYPE:** Appropriate — These are deterministic, analytically/numerically computed spectral‑window (kernel) functions evaluated on a dense frequency grid, not samples from a population. Line plots are the correct representation; none of the distribution‑hiding concerns (bar charts, error bars, summary‑only displays) apply. No pie charts or bar graphs present.

**SAMPLE SIZE:** Not applicable in the statistical sense — There is no sampled data and hence no n to display. The relevant "design parameters" (N = 256, R = 8/(NΔ), L = 7, and the equivalent degrees of freedom ν for each kernel) are given in the caption but are **not** inferable from the figure itself. Consider annotating ν (or at least L) directly in the legend so each curve is self‑identifying.

**COMPARISON:** Valid — All kernels are plotted on a common normalized frequency axis (fNΔ) at a matched design resolution, which is the appropriate basis for comparison. One caveat: panel (a) spans ±10 and panel (b) spans ±25, so eyeball comparison of main‑lobe widths across panels is not 1:1. This is defensible (linear near‑lobe vs. log far‑field) but should be stated in the caption.

**AXIS TRUNCATION:** Present — two instances, one benign and one requiring attention.
- Panel (a): y‑axis begins at 0.00, i.e., not truncated. Good practice. Note the highest gridline/label is 0.20 while the multitaper ripple peaks near 0.135 and the axis top sits near 0.21 — no exaggeration, but the axis extends well above the data.
- Panel (b): the dB axis is truncated at −100 dB, and the Bohman (dashed light‑blue) curve runs **into and along the bottom edge** on the left side. The curve is clipped without any indication, so a reader cannot tell whether it continues downward, plateaus, or hits a numerical floor. This materially affects the central claim about skirt suppression.

**OVERPLOTTING:** Present (mild) — In panel (b) the multitaper curve's dense ripple structure (~30+ oscillations per side) overlaps the untapered curve over much of the range, and near the main lobe in panel (a) five curves crowd between fNΔ ≈ −4 and −3. Additionally, "Multitaper" (solid medium blue) and "Bohman" (dashed light blue) are the same hue family; under grayscale printing or for deuteranopic/protanopic readers these two, plus green/olive, are at risk of being confused.

**MULTIPLE COMPARISONS:** No concern — No inferential annotations, p‑values, or significance brackets appear. Nothing to correct.

**TIME SERIES:** Not applicable — The abscissa is frequency, densely and evenly sampled; no gaps or interpolation across missing points are evident.

**ISSUES:**
- [BLOCKING] Panel (b): the Bohman curve is clipped at the −100 dB axis limit and travels along the bottom boundary without any break marker, arrowhead, or note. The caption's quantitative claim about skirt separation cannot be verified for this curve as drawn.
- [BLOCKING] Caption claim "the skirts differ by 40 dB" is not supported by the plotted curves at all frequencies. At |fNΔ| ≈ 20 the untapered curve sits near −35 dB while Bohman is at or below −100 dB — a separation exceeding 60 dB. Either specify the frequency (or frequency band) at which 40 dB applies, or restate the claim (e.g., "differ by at least 40 dB beyond |fNΔ| ≈ X").
- [ADVISORY] Sample‑size/parameter information (N, L, ν) exists only in the caption; the figure is not self‑contained if reproduced without it.
- [ADVISORY] Color palette places two blues (one solid, one dashed) and a green/olive pair in the same legend; accessibility risk under colorblind simulation and grayscale reproduction.
- [ADVISORY] Differing x‑ranges between panels (±10 vs ±25) are not flagged in the caption and may mislead readers comparing lobe widths across panels.
- [ADVISORY] Legend appears only in panel (a); if panels are reused or cropped separately, panel (b) becomes uninterpretable.

**SUGGESTED FIXES:**
- Extend the panel (b) dB axis (e.g., to −120 or −140 dB) so the Bohman skirt is fully contained, or, if a numerical/precision floor is the limiting factor, draw that floor explicitly as a shaded band or horizontal reference line and state it in the caption.
- Rewrite the skirt statement to be frequency‑qualified and verifiable, e.g., "at |fNΔ| = 15 the skirts span 40 dB, from −25 dB (untapered) to −65 dB (Bohman)"; report the numbers actually read off the plotted curves.
- Add ν (and L where applicable) to each legend entry so the figure stands alone.
- Recolor using a colorblind‑safe, ordered palette and differentiate by linestyle as well as hue (e.g., solid / dashed / dash‑dot / dotted); verify legibility in grayscale.
- Reduce the multitaper ripple overplotting in panel (b) by slightly lowering that curve's alpha or linewidth, or by insetting a zoomed sub‑panel of the ripple region.
- State the differing abscissa ranges explicitly in the caption, and consider a light shaded band marking |fNΔ| ≤ 10 in panel (b) to link the two panels visually.
- Add a brief methods note giving the frequency grid spacing and any numerical smoothing used, so the plotted curves are fully reproducible.

### efigure2.png

---
**PLOT TYPE:** Appropriate — Line/spectral overlay is the correct display for PSD estimates versus frequency; no bar chart, pie chart, or error-bar-hiding construct is involved. One caveat: the figure makes *statistical* claims (bias "floors," variance reduction, degrees of freedom ν) from a **single realization**. A one-realization line plot is the weakest evidence for such claims; a Monte-Carlo summary (mean bias with 5–95% envelope over e.g. 1000 realizations) is the appropriate plot type for the assertions in the caption, with this single-realization panel retained as illustration.

**SAMPLE SIZE:** Not visible — $N=1024$, $L=7/6$, and ν values appear only in the caption, not in or on the figure. More importantly, the number of realizations (apparently 1) is stated nowhere; a reader may mistake the traces for ensemble averages. Individual "data points" are not applicable here, but the frequency grid spacing (Fourier vs. zero-padded/oversampled) is not inferable and affects how the dense oscillations should be read.

**COMPARISON:** Concern — (i) The comparison is inherently *paired* (all estimators computed from the same realization, differenced against the same true PSD), which panel (b) handles correctly; this should be stated explicitly, since a reader could otherwise assume independent runs. (ii) The caption asserts that the $L=6$ dashed curve shows half the bias of $L=7$, but the dashed blue trace is not visually separable from the solid blue trace in (a) and appears to be entirely absent or fully occluded in (b). The central comparative claim of the figure is therefore not verifiable from the figure. (iii) Caption numbers do not match the rendering: "floors 20 dB high" for the green curve reads ≈18 dB in (b), and "$L=7$ floors 10 dB high" is a band centre estimate for a trace whose envelope spans ≈0–14 dB.

**AXIS TRUNCATION:** Present — Not the misleading bar-chart kind, but genuine **data clipping**: in (a) the grey periodogram and the blue dotted adaptive trace run off the bottom of the axis (below ≈ −22 dB) and in (b) the dotted trace is cut at the −10 dB limit near $f\Delta\approx0.45$. Clipping selectively removes the deepest excursions of exactly the estimators whose low-bias behaviour is being praised, which biases visual impression. Panel (b)'s asymmetric limits (−10 to +30) also visually emphasise positive bias over negative.

**OVERPLOTTING:** Present — Severe. Five traces of the same blue hue (solid, dashed, dotted plus their high-variance oscillation envelopes) occupy the same region; above $f\Delta\approx0.25$ the blue band is a solid fill in which no individual estimator can be read, and it overlays the orange and black curves. Line-style differentiation fails at this oscillation density.

**MULTIPLE COMPARISONS:** No concern — no pairwise significance annotations; nothing to correct. (If the ν values are used downstream for confidence intervals at many frequencies, simultaneous-coverage issues should be addressed in text, not here.)

**TIME SERIES:** Not applicable — frequency-domain plot on a complete, evenly spaced grid; no gaps or interpolation across missing points. Confirm no smoothing/decimation was applied for rendering.

**ISSUES:**
- [BLOCKING] The $L=6$ (dashed blue) trace, which carries the paper's headline claim ("dropping the seventh taper halves that"), is indistinguishable from or invisible beneath the $L=7$ trace in (a) and appears missing in (b). The claim cannot be assessed from the figure as drawn.
- [BLOCKING] Data are clipped at the axis limits in both panels (grey and dotted-blue traces in (a); dotted-blue in (b)) without any indication of clipping. Extend limits or add explicit break/clip markers.
- [BLOCKING] Quantitative caption values (20 dB, 10 dB) disagree with the plotted curves (≈18 dB; a 0–14 dB envelope). Reconcile, and define whether the quoted "floor" is a mean, median, or envelope centre.
- [ADVISORY] Bias/variance claims rest on a single realization; no ensemble variability is shown, so the reader cannot distinguish systematic bias from one-draw noise.
- [ADVISORY] Four blue-family traces exceed the perceptual capacity of line-style coding at this oscillation density; also fails colour-vision-deficiency and greyscale-print legibility.
- [ADVISORY] $N$, $R$, $L$, ν, and the number of realizations are caption-only; the figure is not self-contained.
- [ADVISORY] Panel (b) uses asymmetric y-limits about zero, visually privileging positive bias.

**SUGGESTED FIXES:**
- Give each estimator a distinct hue (not three shades/styles of one blue) and verify each trace is visible in both panels; if $L=6$ genuinely coincides with $L=7$ at this scale, add an inset zoom over $f\Delta\in[0.3,0.5]$ where the claimed halving occurs.
- Replace raw oscillating traces above $f\Delta\approx0.2$ with a light envelope/quantile band plus a heavy running-median line per estimator, so bias floors are readable instead of buried in variance.
- Add a Monte-Carlo panel: mean $\hat S(f)-S(f)$ with 5–95% band across ≥1000 realizations, stating the number of realizations in the figure. Keep the single realization clearly labelled "one realization."
- Extend y-limits to contain all plotted data, or mark clipped excursions explicitly; make panel (b) symmetric about 0 (e.g. −20 to +20 dB).
- Annotate $N$, $R$, and ν directly in the panels or legend entries, and state in the caption that all estimators share a single common realization (paired comparison).
- Reconcile the numeric claims with the rendered values and specify the summary statistic used for the quoted floors.

### efigure3.png

---
**PLOT TYPE:** Appropriate — These are exact, deterministic parameter sweeps (kernel/DOF computations), not samples from a population, so line-with-marker plots against a continuous design variable are the correct choice. None of the bar-graph/distribution-hiding concerns apply: there is no central-tendency summary standing in for a distribution. Markers correctly reveal the actual computed grid points rather than implying a continuum.

**SAMPLE SIZE:** Not visible (low severity) — $N=256$ appears only in the caption, not in the figure. Since these are exact computations with "no sampling variability" (as stated), there is no $n$ in the statistical sense and no individual data points to show; the marker positions *are* the complete data. Recommend annotating $N=256$ in-panel so each panel is self-contained.

**COMPARISON:** Valid — The design is a fair comparison: all methods are plotted against the *measured* half-power width rather than against their nominal bandwidth parameters, which is exactly the right way to place methods on a common resolution axis. The dotted reference line at $8/(N\Delta)$ gives an interpretable vertical slice. One contextual concern: in panel (c) the plotted quantity is not the same *kind* of quantity for all curves (side-lobe peak for flat-top kernels vs. main-lobe skirt for Gaussian/parabolic/Welch). This is disclosed in the caption but not in the figure, so a reader scanning panel (c) will over-interpret the Welch/Hann-Gaussian curves as having catastrophic side lobes.

**AXIS TRUNCATION:** None (not misleading) — Panel (a) includes zero. Panel (b) is log-scaled, where a zero origin is impossible and the decade gridlines make the scale explicit. Panel (c) is in dB, an inherently relative logarithmic scale where truncation at $-10$ dB top / $-55$ dB bottom is conventional. No visual exaggeration of differences; the dynamic ranges shown (six decades in b, ~50 dB in c) are genuine.

**OVERPLOTTING:** Present (moderate) — Three distinct problems:
1. In panel (a) below half-power width $\approx 6$, all nine curves converge into a dense bundle where markers overlap and individual methods cannot be traced. Similarly in panel (b) and (c) the upper-left cluster (untapered/Hann-Gaussian/cosine/Welch) is congested.
2. The palette uses three closely related blues (Multitaper $2\alpha-1$, $2\alpha-2$, sine tapers) plus a teal-vs-olive pair that are hard to separate; discrimination relies heavily on marker shape and dash pattern at small sizes. This will fail in grayscale printing and for red–green and blue-spectrum color vision deficiency.
3. Where curves cross (e.g., the blue solid and blue dotted multitaper curves crossing near width 7 in panel c), the identity of each branch after the crossing is ambiguous.

**MULTIPLE COMPARISONS:** No concern — No significance annotations, p-values, or inferential brackets appear anywhere; none would be meaningful for exact deterministic computations. Nothing to correct.

**TIME SERIES:** Concern (analogous issue: uneven sampling and interpolation across gaps) — Although not a time series, the same integrity criterion applies to these swept curves. Two issues:
1. Curves terminate at very different maximum half-power widths (Hann-then-Gaussian stops near 9.5; multitaper and box-smoothed curves near 15–16; Welch extends to 24.5) with no explanation in the figure. A reader cannot tell whether the sweep was infeasible, the method was not computed there, or the curve was truncated for clarity.
2. The Welch curve in panels (b) and (c) is drawn as a single long straight segment from width $\approx 16$ to $\approx 24.5$ with no intermediate markers. In panel (b) that segment spans a region where every other curve is changing rapidly, so the flat interpolation is an artifact of sparse sampling, not a demonstrated plateau. The same applies to the long final segment in panel (a), which linearly interpolates a jump from $\nu\approx 38$ to $\nu\approx 60$.

**ISSUES:**
- [ADVISORY] Panel (c) mixes two physically different quantities (side-lobe peak vs. main-lobe skirt) on one axis; only the caption discloses this, so the figure alone is misleading about Welch, Hann-Gaussian, and cosine-parabola performance.
- [ADVISORY] Long unsampled interpolation segments, most conspicuously the Welch curve from width $\approx 16$ to $\approx 24.5$ in all three panels, imply behavior that was not computed. Interpolated flatness in panel (b) is not evidence of a plateau.
- [ADVISORY] Unequal and unexplained x-range coverage across methods; readers may misread a curve's endpoint as a performance limit.
- [ADVISORY] Overplotting and low color separability in the convergent low-width region of panel (a) and the upper-left cluster of panels (b) and (c); three near-identical blues plus a teal/olive pair are not robustly distinguishable, and the figure is not grayscale- or CVD-safe.
- [ADVISORY] $N=256$ and the meaning of the dotted vertical line are documented only in the caption, leaving panels non-self-contained.
- [ADVISORY] Panel (b) has no minor-tick or minor-gridline guidance between decades, making it hard to read intermediate values across six decades.

**SUGGESTED FIXES:**
- Add denser sweep points where curves are sparse, especially the Welch curve between widths 16 and 24.5 in all panels; if those points are genuinely not computable, break the line and show the endpoint as an isolated marker rather than connecting across the gap.
- In panel (c), visually distinguish the three main-lobe-skirt curves from the true side-lobe curves, for example by plotting them with a lighter weight or open markers and adding a short in-panel note such as "dashed/pale = main-lobe skirt, not a side lobe."
- Add a one-line in-panel annotation stating why each curve ends where it does (e.g., "curves end at the largest feasible bandwidth parameter"), or extend all sweeps to a common maximum width.
- Recolor to a CVD-safe, perceptually ordered palette: reserve a single hue family with varied dash patterns for the three multitaper variants but increase lightness separation between them, and move the teal/olive pair further apart in hue. Verify legibility in grayscale.
- Consider direct end-of-line labeling for the well-separated curves at the right side of each panel, reducing dependence on the legend and on color matching.
- Add an inset or a companion panel zooming on half-power widths 1–6 in panel (a), where all nine curves currently overlap, so the low-resolution regime is readable.
- Add minor-tick marks (and optionally faint minor gridlines) to the log axis of panel (b), and state $N=256$ plus the identity of the dotted line ("$8/(N\Delta)$, Table 2") inside at least one panel.
- Consider a fourth panel or a summary table giving the exact values of all three metrics at the $8/(N\Delta)$ reference line, since that vertical slice is the figure's main quantitative takeaway and is currently read off by eye through a dense curve bundle.
---

### efigure4.png

---
**PLOT TYPE:** Appropriate — This is a methods/analytical figure: rows 1–2 show deterministic taper (window) functions, row 3 shows eigen-weights of a deterministic matrix $Q$, and row 4 shows analytically computed kernels. None of these are samples from a population, so the usual distribution-hiding objection to bar graphs does **not** apply to panels (i)–(l): each bar is a single exact eigenvalue ratio, not a mean of replicates. Bars are anchored at zero and the quantity is a proportion of a sum, so a bar (ladder) representation is defensible. A lollipop/stem plot would be marginally cleaner for discrete index $i$ but is not required.

**SAMPLE SIZE:** Visible (in the relevant sense) — There is no statistical $n$. The analogous quantity, the number of tapers $L$, is directly inferable by counting bars in (i)–(l) (7, 12, 11, 7) and is stated for column 4 in the title ("Multitaper, $L=7$"). Recommend stating $L$ (and $N$, $NW$/$R$, segment length and overlap for Welch, smoothing bandwidth for column 2) in every column title or in the caption, since these are the free parameters that make the columns comparable.

**COMPARISON:** Concern — The four columns are intended as a like-for-like comparison of spectral-estimation strategies, but the vertical scales and normalisations are not matched:
- Row 1: $y$ limits are ~0.21, ~0.13, 1.0 and 0.2, and panel (c) is additionally normalised as $h_{k-n}/\max h$ while (a), (b), (d) are not. A reader scanning the row will misread relative taper amplitudes.
- Row 1 panel (b) plots $h_k\cos(2\pi f'k\Delta)$, a different object from $h_{k-n}$ in (a)/(c) and $h_k^{(i)}$ in (d). The row therefore mixes three quantities under one implied heading.
- Rows 3: the $x$-axis is extended to ~12 in all panels so ladders of different length can be compared — good — but the empty region in (i) and (l) is ambiguous between "weight is zero" and "no such taper exists".

**AXIS TRUNCATION:** None (misleading) — All bar axes in (i)–(l) begin at 0.00, which is correct practice. The dB panels (m)–(p) are floored at −80 dB; this is conventional and the caption states values are relative to peak, but note that the sidelobe floor of (p) sits near −40 dB and (m) dips below the axis limit, so the clipping of (m)'s nulls should be acknowledged as clipping rather than read as data reaching the axis.

**OVERPLOTTING:** Present — Panels (a) and (c) superimpose many curves of the same hue distinguished only by lightness, with substantial mutual occlusion; in (a) the seven overlapping segment windows are indistinguishable and unlabelled, and in (c) only three or four of the sliding-sinc windows are visually separable. Panels (f), (g), (h) rely on legends located in (d) and (e) only, so the $i=1\ldots4$ identity of curves must be inferred from lightness matching across panels — colour-vision-deficient and greyscale readers cannot do this reliably.

**MULTIPLE COMPARISONS:** No concern — No inferential annotations, p-values, or significance brackets appear anywhere in the figure. The numbers reported ($\nu$, half-power widths, out-of-band power fractions) are deterministic characterisations, not test statistics.

**TIME SERIES:** Not applicable — The abscissa $k$ is a sample index within a fixed-length window ($0$–$256$) with no gaps or uneven spacing, and $fN\Delta$ is a frequency axis. There is no longitudinal interpolation risk.

**ISSUES:**
- [ADVISORY] Row 1 mixes three different plotted quantities ($h_{k-n}$, $h_k\cos(2\pi f'k\Delta)$, $h_{k-n}/\max h$, $h_k^{(i)}$) across four panels arranged as a single comparative row, with four different $y$ scales and one panel peak-normalised. Cross-column amplitude comparison is invited by the layout but is not valid.
- [ADVISORY] Panels (a) and (c) suffer heavy curve occlusion and carry no legend; the number and identity of superimposed curves cannot be recovered from the figure.
- [ADVISORY] Legends for the eigen-taper index $i$ appear only in (d) and (e); panels (f)–(h) are unlabelled and depend on lightness transfer across panels and hues.
- [ADVISORY] Empty $x$-range in (i) and (l) is ambiguous between "zero weight" and "taper does not exist".
- [ADVISORY] Key parameters that determine every curve shown (window length $N$, Welch segment length and overlap, smoother bandwidth, sinc bandwidth, time–bandwidth product / $R$) are not stated in the figure or caption, so the comparison is not reproducible from the figure alone.
- [ADVISORY] Numerical claims in the caption (half-power widths; out-of-band power percentages) are not readable from the panels; the dotted lines mark $|f|=R/2$ whereas the out-of-band fractions are quoted for $|f|\le R$, an easy source of reader confusion.

**SUGGESTED FIXES:**
- Harmonise row 1: use a common $y$ scale and a common normalisation (e.g. plot all tapers with unit energy, or all peak-normalised), and if panel (b) must show the modulated window, separate it visually or retitle the row so it is not read as the same quantity.
- Add an in-panel legend or direct line labels to (a) and (c); reduce occlusion by offsetting curves vertically, plotting a light grey "all curves" underlay with two or three highlighted exemplars, or using a small inset that shows a single window plus the overlap pattern.
- Replicate the $i=1\ldots4$ legend in (f), (g), (h), or use a shared legend strip for row 2; supplement lightness coding with line style (solid/dashed/dotted/dash-dot) so the four eigen-tapers remain distinguishable in greyscale.
- In (i)–(l), draw open/zero-height markers or a light shaded region beyond the last taper, and annotate $L=7$, $12$, $11$, $7$ directly in each panel next to $\nu$.
- Consider converting (i)–(l) to stem/lollipop plots, which represent a discrete index more honestly than area-encoded bars, and state explicitly in the caption that these are exact eigenvalues of $Q$ with no sampling variability, so readers do not expect error bars.
- State $N$, $\Delta$, $R$ (or $NW$), Welch segment length/overlap and smoother bandwidth in the caption; clarify whether the dotted lines are at $|f|=R/2$ while the quoted leakage fractions use $|f|\le R$, and if so add a second pair of lines or change one convention.
- Note in the caption that (m) is clipped at the −80 dB floor so that nulls extending below the axis are not misread.

### figure1.png

---
**PLOT TYPE:** Appropriate — Line plots on (a) a dB–frequency axis and (b) a log-magnitude difference axis are the correct choice for continuous spectral estimates; no bar/pie misuse. Panel (c) is a log–log convergence plot, also appropriate. One structural caveat: panel (a) deliberately overlays three curves that are numerically near-identical, so it functions as a *coincidence* demonstration, not a comparison — the visual "agreement" is entirely determined by plot order (thick blue drawn first, green dashed, pink dotted on top). The scientific content of (a) is therefore carried almost entirely by panel (b). This is defensible but should be stated explicitly.

**SAMPLE SIZE:** Not visible — The figure contains no in-panel indication that this is a single 8 s epoch of a single channel from a single subject. $N=1024$, one epoch, $R=1$ Hz, $\alpha=4$, and $\nu=17$ appear only in the caption. Because $n=1$ record, panels (b) and (c) report single-realization quantities (a max over frequency for one data segment) with no indication of how much they would vary across epochs, channels, or subjects. For an $n=1$ demonstration, the underlying "data points" (i.e., the individual records) cannot be shown by definition — but the reader should be told that no replication is displayed.

**COMPARISON:** Concern — The three estimators are computed on the *same* record, i.e., these are paired (repeated-measures) comparisons. Panel (b) correctly handles this by plotting pointwise differences rather than overlaid magnitudes, which is the right paired visualization. However, panel (c) collapses a paired, frequency-resolved difference into a single scalar maximum per $N_s$; the maximum is a worst-case order statistic and is sensitive to record length and to any spectral outlier, so its convergence rate is a weaker claim than an RMS or median difference would be. Recommend showing both a central and a worst-case summary.

**AXIS TRUNCATION:** None (misleading) — Panel (a) does not start at zero, but the ordinate is already logarithmic (dB), so "zero" is not meaningful; no exaggeration results. Panels (b) and (c) are log axes spanning many decades and are not compressed or clipped in a way that flatters the result — if anything the 15-decade span in (b) makes the box-vs-multitaper agreement look *less* impressive relative to the sliding-sinc trace. Minor: (b) has ticks/gridlines only at $10^0,10^{-5},10^{-10},10^{-15}$, which makes it hard to read the pink trace's level (caption says 0.06 dB); the $3\times10^{-13}$ dB claim also cannot be verified against the visible green trace, which appears to sit nearer $10^{-14}$–$10^{-13}$ with excursions.

**OVERPLOTTING:** Present — Two distinct instances. (1) Panel (a): three curves are mutually occluding by construction; the blue and green traces are essentially invisible beneath the pink dotted overlay across most of the band. (2) Panel (b): both difference traces are dense, high-frequency-content line plots at ~1000 frequency bins compressed into ~1000 px, so the traces render as filled bands and individual excursions/spikes are not resolvable. Transparency, decimation, or an envelope/quantile band would convey the level and spread more honestly.

**MULTIPLE COMPARISONS:** No concern — No inferential comparisons, p-values, or significance annotations are present. Panel (c), however, reports a maximum over ~1000 frequency bins for each of 5 window lengths; the maximum of many values is upward-biased and its sampling behaviour is not that of a mean. This is a selection effect rather than a multiplicity error, but it should be acknowledged.

**TIME SERIES:** Not applicable — Both abscissae are frequency or window length. Panel (c)'s $N_s/N$ values are dyadic (1, 2, 4, 8, 16) and correctly placed on a log axis, so the uneven spacing is not misleading. The straight segments joining the five points do imply a continuum of intermediate $N_s$ values that were not evaluated; markers are shown, which mitigates this.

**ISSUES:**
  - [BLOCKING] Panel (c) axis label is $N_s/N$ (dimensionless ratio) while the caption states the abscissa is "the window length $N_s$". These are not the same quantity. Resolve the mismatch, and state explicitly which is plotted.
  - [BLOCKING] The $1/N_s$ guide line does not appear to have slope $-1$ consistent with the plotted data over the full range: the data fall by roughly a factor of 24 (≈1.2 dB → ≈0.05 dB) across a 16-fold increase in $N_s$, i.e. slightly *steeper* than $1/N_s$, while the dotted guide spans a factor of ~20. Verify that the guide line is exactly $\propto N_s^{-1}$ on these log–log axes and that the caption's claim of $1/N_s$ convergence is supported rather than an eyeballed fit. If the asymptotic rate is claimed, state which points it is fitted to.
  - [BLOCKING] Sample size and generalizability are not inferable from the figure. The quantitative agreement figures in the caption ($3\times10^{-13}$ dB, 0.06 dB) are single-realization results from one 8 s epoch of one channel of one subject and are presented without any indication of variability. As stated, a reader may take them as properties of the estimators rather than of this record.
  - [ADVISORY] Panel (a) is uninformative as a comparison because the three curves are indistinguishable by design and are occluded in a fixed drawing order. A reader cannot verify from (a) that all three were plotted.
  - [ADVISORY] Panel (b) has too few tick marks to read either trace's level; the caption's numerical agreement values cannot be checked against the plot.
  - [ADVISORY] Panel (c) summarizes each condition by a maximum over ~1000 frequency bins, a worst-case order statistic that is upward-biased and unstable, with no central-tendency companion.
  - [ADVISORY] Overplotting in (b) renders both traces as solid bands; the distribution of differences across frequency is obscured.
  - [ADVISORY] Data provenance ("the authors' archive") is vague. Even for a methods demonstration, state acquisition details and whether the record can be shared, since every number in the caption is specific to it.
  - [ADVISORY] Green/pink/blue line encoding plus dash patterns is reasonable but green-vs-pink at thin line widths may be hard for some readers; dash patterns carry most of the load, which is acceptable.

**SUGGESTED FIXES:**
  - Correct the (c) axis label/caption mismatch and confirm the $1/N_s$ reference line's slope is exactly $-1$; annotate the fitted range or label it "slope $-1$ guide" rather than implying a fitted rate.
  - Add in-panel annotation of the key parameters: $N=1024$, one epoch, one channel, $R=1$ Hz, $\alpha=4$, $\nu=17$. State in the caption that $n=1$ record and that no across-record variability is shown.
  - Demonstrate robustness: repeat panels (b) and (c) for several epochs/channels (e.g., 20 epochs) and plot the family of curves, or a median with min–max envelope, so the convergence claim is not resting on a single realization.
  - Replace or supplement the maximum in (c) with an RMS or median absolute difference across frequency, plotted alongside the maximum; the two together bound the behaviour and the max's instability becomes visible.
  - Make panel (a) honest about occlusion: either add a zoomed inset over a narrow band where all three line styles are separately visible, or apply small deliberate vertical offsets with the offset stated in the caption, or state plainly that the curves coincide to within line width and direct the reader to (b).
  - In (b), add minor ticks/gridlines at each decade, and reduce overplotting with partial transparency, decimation, or a quantile envelope; mark the caption's quoted maxima ($3\times10^{-13}$ dB, 0.06 dB) with horizontal reference lines so the numbers are verifiable from the figure.
  - Expand the data provenance statement (subject count, recording conditions, filtering, availability).

### figure2.png

---
**PLOT TYPE:** Appropriate — These are line plots of continuous spectral estimates versus frequency, which is the correct idiom; none of the bar/pie/error-bar concerns apply. Two caveats: (i) the periodogram in (a)/(b) is a set of 512 discrete Fourier ordinates, and joining them with a solid line implies a continuous function it is not — at this line density the traces read as a filled band rather than as data; (ii) the side-lobe "envelope" curves in (e)–(h) are a *derived* summary drawn in the same style as the true kernel, i.e. two different kinds of object share one visual encoding.

**SAMPLE SIZE:** Not visible — "one realization ($N=1024$)" appears only in the caption. The effective replication of the figure is n = 1 per panel; nothing in the graphic indicates that the visible roughness is a single draw rather than a reproducible feature. The $\nu$ annotations are a partial, indirect substitute for a variance display (they are the only in-figure quantity carrying uncertainty information), but a reader cannot tell from the figure whether the peak heights in (c)/(d) are typical or a lucky draw.

**COMPARISON:** Concern — Panels (a)–(d) are, by construction, *paired* comparisons: the same realization of the same process processed four ways. This pairing is the whole point of the figure, yet it is shown as four side-by-side unpaired panels, so the reader must eyeball across ~500 px of white space to judge whether a given wiggle was removed by tapering or merely relocated. A single overlay (or a difference-from-true-PSD panel) would make the paired structure explicit. Secondary concern: panel (a) draws the estimate in grey and (b)–(d) in orange, so a colour change coincides with a method change, inviting the reading that (a) is a different class of object than (b)–(d) when only the taper differs.

**AXIS TRUNCATION:** Present — Two separate instances, one of which is substantive.
 - Top row: the $\hat S(f)$ axis stops at $-40$ dB. In (b) the tapered periodogram visibly runs into the bottom of the frame; excursions below $-40$ dB are clipped and invisible. Because the figure's argument is about *low-level* leakage on a 65 dB background, silently discarding the deepest troughs flatters the tapered estimate and understates its variance. This is the one truncation that materially affects interpretation.
 - Insets: the zoom windows are $0.39$–$0.41$ in (e), (f), (g) but $0.36$–$0.44$ in (h) — a 4× wider window drawn at identical physical size. The main lobe in (h) therefore looks comparable in width to (g) when it is in fact ~6× wider. The axis labels do disclose this, but the visual impression contradicts the numbers.
 - Not misleading: the $-150$ dB floor in (e)–(h) and the common $0$–$0.5$ frequency range are consistent across panels and fine.

**OVERPLOTTING:** Present — In (a) and (b) the estimate oscillates on the Nyquist grid, so adjacent ordinates are sub-pixel apart and the trace saturates into a solid mass, especially for $f\Delta > 0.25$. The *density* of ordinates at a given level — which is what distinguishes a $\nu=2$ estimate from a biased one — is unreadable. In (f) the same problem afflicts the side-lobe structure between $f\Delta=0$ and $0.3$.

**MULTIPLE COMPARISONS:** No concern — No inferential annotations, brackets, or p-values are present; none are appropriate here.

**TIME SERIES:** Not applicable — The abscissa is frequency, densely and evenly sampled, with no gaps. (The analogous integrity question, interpolation across a sparse grid, is addressed under PLOT TYPE above.)

**ISSUES:**
 - [BLOCKING] Data clipping at the $-40$ dB axis limit in panel (b) (and possibly a). Values below the frame are silently lost in a figure whose thesis concerns dynamic range and leakage floors. Either extend the axis to contain all plotted ordinates or state explicitly in the caption that the estimate is clipped and by how much.
 - [BLOCKING] Numerical inconsistency between figure and caption: panel (d) is annotated $\nu = 98$ while the caption states $\nu = 98.4$. Similarly the caption lists half-power widths "0.8, 1.2, 8.2 and 47.8$/(N\Delta)$" against panel annotations $R = 8/(N\Delta)$ and $48/(N\Delta)$ — the relationship between the design parameter $R$ and the realised half-power width is not made explicit, so the two number sets appear to disagree. Reconcile and use one convention.
 - [ADVISORY] Inset zoom windows are not on a common scale ((h) is 4× wider than (e)–(g)) while being drawn at equal physical width, visually suppressing the very main-lobe broadening the panel is meant to demonstrate.
 - [ADVISORY] n = 1 realization is disclosed only in the caption; no indication in the figure that the traces are a single draw, and no display of realization-to-realization variability.
 - [ADVISORY] Overplotting in (a), (b), (f) obscures ordinate density; the saturated mass cannot be distinguished from a genuinely thick band.
 - [ADVISORY] Two distinct kinds of curve (true kernel vs. envelope of side-lobe peaks) share one line style in (e)–(h); disclosed in caption text only.
 - [ADVISORY] The in-panel legend in (a) ("True PSD" / "Estimate") is set in black and light grey; the grey "Estimate" label is low-contrast and will likely fail at print size or in greyscale reproduction. It also appears only in (a) though it governs all four top panels.
 - [ADVISORY] Colour switches from grey (a) to orange (b)–(d) without semantic justification, implying a category difference where only the taper changes.

**SUGGESTED FIXES:**
 - Extend the top-row ordinate to the minimum plotted value (e.g. $-60$ dB) so no ordinate is clipped; if the limit is retained for compactness, annotate "estimate clipped at $-40$ dB" in the panel.
 - Use a single zoom half-width for all four insets, or keep the wider window in (h) but scale its drawn width proportionally (or add a shared scale bar) so main-lobe widths compare correctly by eye.
 - Reconcile $\nu$ and half-power-width values between annotations and caption; state once whether quoted widths are design parameters ($R$) or realised half-power widths.
 - Add a small multi-realization element: overplot 5–10 additional independent realizations at low opacity, or shade the theoretical $\chi^2_\nu$ pointwise 95% interval about the true PSD. This converts the implicit $\nu$ annotation into a visible statement of variability and makes the $\nu = 2 \to 98$ progression self-evident.
 - Reduce overplotting by drawing the periodogram with ~40–50% opacity and hairline width, or plot the ordinates as fine dots rather than a connected line, making the discrete nature of the estimate and its density legible.
 - Distinguish the side-lobe envelope from the true kernel by style (e.g. envelope as a dashed or lighter line) and label it once in-panel, rather than relying on the caption sentence.
 - Use one colour for the estimate across (a)–(d); reserve colour changes for genuine category differences.
 - Set the legend in (a) in black text with a short colour swatch per entry rather than colour-coded text, and either repeat it or move it to a figure-level legend.
 - Consider adding a difference panel ($\hat S - S$ in dB) or overlaying the four estimates on shared axes, so the paired same-realization structure of (a)–(d) is visible directly rather than inferred across panels.

### figure3.png

---
**PLOT TYPE:** Appropriate — Panel (a) is a trace plot of a single 1024-sample realization, which is the correct display for a record/taper illustration. Panel (b) is a line-and-marker plot of a summary statistic (RMS error) against a continuous tuning parameter (*RNΔ*); this is the right family of plot. No bar charts or pie charts are present, so the usual bar-graph concerns do not apply. The relevant analogue of the "bar-with-error-bar" problem does appear in panel (b): each plotted marker is the mean over 300 realizations with no depiction of the across-realization distribution.

**SAMPLE SIZE:** Not visible — The ensemble size (300 independent realizations) and the record length (*N* = 1024) appear only in the caption. *N* = 1024 is at least inferable from the *k* axis of panel (a); the 300 realizations underlying panel (b) are not inferable from the figure at all. Individual realizations are not shown in panel (b), nor is any summary of their spread. With n = 300 the omission of individual points is defensible, but the omission of *any* dispersion indicator is not.

**COMPARISON:** Concern — The two curves in panel (b) ("Total, whole band" and "Total, peak region") are, per the caption, computed from the same 300 realizations and are therefore paired at every value of *R*. They are displayed as two independent unpaired curves. The scientific claim drawn from them — that the optimum *R* is smaller when only the peak region is scored — is a within-realization difference and would be far better supported by plotting the paired difference (or the distribution of per-realization argmin) than by comparing two ensemble means by eye. The "Bias" and "Noise" decomposition curves are also compared against the total without any indication of whether bias + noise reproduces the total in quadrature as drawn.

**AXIS TRUNCATION:** None — Panel (b) *y* begins at 0 dB and *x* at 0; panel (a) is symmetric about 0 in *x_k*. No exaggeration of differences by truncation. One related concern, logged below, is that the dashed taper (a dimensionless 0–1 window) is plotted on the *x_k* amplitude axis after being rescaled to the record's peak amplitude, so its ordinate values are not readable as anything and invite misreading as a signal envelope.

**OVERPLOTTING:** Present — Two separate instances. (i) Panel (a) draws ~1024 samples of a strongly oscillatory process across ~800 px, so adjacent samples collide; the visible envelope is partly a rendering artifact and individual extrema may be drawn inaccurately depending on the rasterization/decimation path. (ii) The orange tapered record is superimposed on the black record, and over the central ~75% of the window (where the taper is unity) they are identical, so the black trace is entirely hidden. A reader cannot tell whether the black trace exists there or whether the two differ slightly.

**MULTIPLE COMPARISONS:** No concern — No pairwise significance annotations, brackets, asterisks, or p-values are present. Nothing to correct. (If the paired whole-band vs. peak-region comparison is later tested at multiple *R* values, correction across *R* would become relevant, but the figure as drawn makes no inferential claim.)

**TIME SERIES:** Concern — Panel (a) is a discrete sampled record plotted as a continuous polyline, i.e. linear interpolation between samples. Intervals are even and there are no missing timepoints, so there is no gap-interpolation problem, but the continuous rendering of a heavily aliased discrete series is a mild integrity issue for a figure whose whole purpose is to teach spectral estimation.

**ISSUES:**
- [BLOCKING] Panel (b) reports ensemble means over 300 realizations with no depiction of sampling variability. The figure's central claims — that the whole-band total has a "broad minimum," that the minimum for the peak region sits at smaller *R*, and that ν = 17 is "just right" while ν = 2 and ν = 98 are not — all depend on whether the curve-to-curve and point-to-point differences exceed the across-realization scatter. As drawn, a reader cannot assess this. The caption's statement that "the curves are ensemble averages and carry no error bars" is commendably transparent but does not remedy the loss of information.
- [ADVISORY] Ensemble size n = 300 is not shown anywhere in the figure. A reader viewing panel (b) without the caption has no idea whether it rests on 3 or 3000 realizations.
- [ADVISORY] Unpaired presentation of paired quantities: the whole-band and peak-region error curves share realizations, and the argmin difference between them is a within-realization effect that the current display cannot support.
- [ADVISORY] The dashed taper is rescaled to the record's peak amplitude and plotted on the *x_k* axis. Its plotted ordinate is therefore arbitrary and reads as a signal envelope rather than as a window function taking values in [0, 1].
- [ADVISORY] Panel (a): the black record is completely occluded by the orange tapered record over the central portion of the window, so the effect of the taper — the point of the panel — is only visible in the two end regions.
- [ADVISORY] Panel (a): a 1024-sample oscillatory series drawn as a solid polyline at this pixel width is aliased; apparent peak amplitudes are rendering-dependent, and the dashed taper is scaled to one of those possibly-misdrawn peaks.
- [ADVISORY] The relationship between the grey "Bias" and "Noise" curves and the black total is asserted but not made verifiable; if total = sqrt(bias² + noise²) or similar, this should be stated so the reader can check the decomposition against the plotted curves.
- [ADVISORY] Units are dB throughout the *y* axis of (b), so visual distances are ratios, not differences in error. Worth an explicit note, since the "broad minimum" looks flatter on a dB scale than it would on a linear one.

**SUGGESTED FIXES:**
- Add a dispersion band to both curves in panel (b): a shaded ±1 SD or, preferably, a 10th–90th percentile envelope of the per-realization RMS error at each *R*. With n = 300 this is cheap and will show immediately how well-determined the minimum is.
- Alternatively or additionally, overplot ~20–30 individual per-realization curves as thin, high-transparency grey lines behind the two ensemble means; this conveys both the level and the shape variability of the error-vs-*R* trade-off.
- Add an inset or third panel showing the distribution of the per-realization optimal *R* (the argmin) for the whole band and for the peak region — e.g. two overlaid histograms or a paired dot/violin plot with connecting lines. This directly and honestly supports the claim that the peak-region optimum is smaller, and turns an unpaired eyeball comparison into a paired one.
- Print "n = 300 realizations" and "N = 1024" directly in panel (b) (and *N* in panel (a)) rather than relying on the caption.
- Give the taper its own right-hand axis labelled 0 to 1, or plot the taper in a thin strip above panel (a), so its ordinate is meaningful and it is not confounded with the record's amplitude.
- In panel (a), offset the tapered record vertically from the raw record, or plot the raw record in a light grey with a thicker line and the tapered record thin on top, so the raw trace remains visible where the two coincide.
- Reduce the line width in panel (a), or plot the record as an envelope (min/max per pixel column) plus a zoomed inset of ~100 samples showing the individual samples as markers, so the reader sees that the data are discrete and that the drawn trace is a summary.
- State the total-error decomposition explicitly in the caption (e.g. "total = sqrt(bias² + noise²)") so the three curves can be checked against one another.
- Note in the caption that the *y* axis is logarithmic in error (dB), so apparent flatness of the minimum should be interpreted on a ratio scale.
- Confirm in the caption whether the same 300 realizations are reused across all values of *R*; if so, state that the plotted points along each curve are correlated, which matters for any claim about the location of the minimum.

### figure4.png

---
**Assessment context:** This is a deterministic/analytical figure (Slepian tapers, spectral kernels, eigenvalue ladder). It contains no sampled data, no estimates with uncertainty, and no group comparisons, so most statistical-visualization criteria are not applicable. Review below focuses on the criteria that do apply (axis integrity, interpolation across discrete indices, parameter visibility) plus caption–axis consistency.

PLOT TYPE:          Appropriate — Line plots for continuous functions of $k$ and $f$ (a–c) and a marker+line plot for a discrete eigenvalue sequence (d) are the correct choices. No bar charts, pie charts, or error-bar summaries are used; no distributional hiding occurs because these are exact computed quantities, not samples.

SAMPLE SIZE:        Visible (by analogy) — There is no statistical $n$. The governing parameters ($N=256$, $R=8/(N\Delta)$, $\alpha=4$, $L$) are the analogue; $N=256$ is inferable from panel (a)'s abscissa and $L$ is in the panel (c) legend, but $\alpha$ (hence $2\alpha=8$, $2\alpha-1=7$) is only defined in the caption/text, not on the figure. Panel (d) marks $L=2\alpha-1$ and $2\alpha$ without stating $\alpha$'s value.

COMPARISON:         Valid — Panel (c) is an inherently "paired"/nested comparison (running partial sums of the same kernel family) and is correctly shown as overlaid curves on a common axis with a monotone colour ramp, plus the box reference. Panel (b) correctly overlays the three kernels on identical axes. No unpaired display of paired quantities.

AXIS TRUNCATION:    Present (verify, likely benign) —
 - (a) $y$ limited to $[-0.20, 0.20]$; the plotted extrema ($\approx\pm0.12$) fit, but there appears to be curve material running along the lower axis line at $y=-0.20$. Confirm no curve is clipped at the frame; if a segment lies exactly on the limit it will read as a spurious flat line.
 - (b) $y$ extends to $\approx0.65$ with a maximum of $0.49$; harmless headroom for the legend.
 - (c) $y$ starts at 0 (good); $y_{\max}=0.20$ vs. plateau $\approx0.125$ — again headroom for the legend, not exaggeration.
 - (d) log $y$ spans to $10^{-16}$ while the smallest datum is $\approx2\times10^{-10}$ — six decades of empty axis. This is the inverse of the usual offence (it visually *compresses* rather than exaggerates), but it wastes dynamic range and hints at values near machine epsilon that are not present.
 No truncation that inflates apparent differences; none of the panels is a bar chart.

OVERPLOTTING:       None — Curve counts are low (≤3 in b, ≤6 in c), colours are distinguishable, and (d) has 17 well-separated markers. Minor caveat: the lightest ramp colour ($L=1$) in (c) has low contrast against white and may vanish in print or on projection.

MULTIPLE COMPARISONS: No concern — No hypothesis tests, p-values, or significance annotations appear.

TIME SERIES:        Concern (analogue) — Panel (d) joins the discrete eigenvalue index $i=1,\dots,17$ with a continuous solid line. $1-\lambda_i$ is defined only at integers, so the connecting line is interpolation across undefined domain. The intervals are even and no points are missing, so it is not misleading about gaps, but the line implies a continuum that does not exist. Also confirm whether the sequence truly terminates at $i=17$ or is truncated from $i=1,\dots,N$; if truncated, that should be stated.

ISSUES:
  - [BLOCKING] Caption–axis label mismatch, panel (b): the caption writes the kernels as $|H^{(i)}(f)|^2/\Delta$ while the ordinate is labelled $|H^{(i)}(f)|^2/N\Delta^2$. These differ by a factor $N\Delta$. One of the two is wrong, and a reader cannot recover the intended normalisation.
  - [BLOCKING] Caption–axis label mismatch, panel (c): the caption describes the plotted quantity as $\sum_{i\le L}\lambda_i|H^{(i)}(f)|^2/2\alpha\Delta$, but the ordinate reads $\mathcal{K}(f)/N\Delta$. If $\mathcal{K}(f)$ is defined elsewhere as that sum, the figure must say so; as it stands the normalisation is ambiguous and the claimed agreement with the box (height $\approx0.125 = 1/8$) cannot be checked by the reader.
  - [ADVISORY] Possible curve clipping at the lower frame of panel (a); a flat artefact appears to coincide with $y=-0.20$.
  - [ADVISORY] Panel (d) uses a solid connecting line through a discrete index, implying interpolation over a non-existent continuum.
  - [ADVISORY] Panel (d) log ordinate extends ~6 decades below the smallest plotted value; the lower limit ($10^{-16}$) is at the scale of double-precision round-off, which may invite misreading of numerical noise floors.
  - [ADVISORY] $\alpha$ is never given numerically on the figure, yet panel (d) annotates $L=2\alpha-1$ and $2\alpha$; the reader must consult the caption/text to learn these are 7 and 8.
  - [ADVISORY] The dashed "Box" reference in (c) is stated in the caption to be indistinguishable from the $L=N$ curve at this scale — i.e. the figure cannot support the claim it is asked to illustrate. No residual/inset is provided.
  - [ADVISORY] Lightest blue ($L=1$) in the sequential ramp has marginal contrast; the ramp is also not perceptually uniform.
  - [ADVISORY] Panels (b) and (d) use dotted/dashed vertical reference lines for different purposes (band edge vs. taper counts) with no visual distinction in style logic; minor risk of conflation.

SUGGESTED FIXES:
  - Reconcile the caption formulae with the ordinate labels in (b) and (c); state one normalisation and use it in both places. Explicitly define $\mathcal{K}(f)$ in the caption.
  - Inspect and extend the panel (a) $y$-limits (e.g. $[-0.21,0.21]$) or set clipping off, and confirm no curve lies on the frame.
  - In (d), plot markers only, or markers with a light dashed guide, and state in the caption that the line is a visual guide across a discrete index. Note whether $i$ is truncated at 17 out of $N=256$.
  - Tighten the (d) ordinate to roughly $10^{-11}$–$10^{0}$, or add a note that values below $\sim10^{-15}$ would be at numerical precision.
  - Add the numerical value of $\alpha$ (=4) as an on-figure annotation in (d), e.g. "$2\alpha-1=7$" and "$2\alpha=8$" in the legend labels.
  - Substantiate the "cannot be distinguished from the box" claim with an inset showing $\mathcal{K}(f)/N\Delta$ minus the box (or a log-scale residual) over $|fN\Delta|\le5$; otherwise soften the wording to "agrees to within line width".
  - Darken the lowest ramp step in (c) or switch to a perceptually uniform sequential map, and place the $L=1$ curve on top so it is not occluded.
  - Report on-figure the parameter set ($N$, $\alpha$, $R$) in a small annotation so each panel is self-contained.
  - State in the caption that all curves are exact/analytical computations, not estimates from data, so that no reader expects uncertainty bands.
---

### figure5.png

---

**Context note:** This figure contains no empirical/sampled measurements. Panels (a)–(d) plot deterministic taper sequences and (e)–(h) plot deterministic eigen-weight ladders computed from those banks. Consequently most distributional-integrity criteria (sample size, pairing, error bars, multiple comparisons) are formally not applicable; the checks below are applied in their analogous form (rendering fidelity of discrete sequences, exhaustiveness vs. subsetting, axis comparability).

PLOT TYPE:          Appropriate — Line plots for taper shapes vs. sample index $k$ are correct. The bar charts in (e)–(h) are also appropriate here: $c_i$ is a discrete, exact (non-stochastic) quantity indexed by integer $i$, there is no underlying distribution to hide, and no error bars are shown or implied. The usual "bars conceal the distribution" objection does not apply. Bars are preferable to a line here because $i$ is not a continuous variable.

SAMPLE SIZE:        Not visible — The number of members in each bank ($L$ or effective count) is not annotated in (e)–(h). Panel (e) appears to have 7 bars, (f) ~13, (g) ~12, (h) 7, but the reader must count them against x-ticks placed only at 5 and 10. The caption states $L=7$ for (d)/(h) but not the count for (f)/(g), where the ladder decays smoothly and the truncation point is a choice rather than a property. For (b) and (c) the caption says "four of a continuum" / "four positions shown" — i.e. (a)–(d) show a *subset*, and which members are shown is not recoverable from the panels themselves.

COMPARISON:         Concern — Cross-panel comparison of taper amplitude is not valid as drawn: (a) is plotted in raw $h_{k-n}$ units (0–0.21), (b) in $h_k\cos(\cdot)$ units (−0.075–0.125), (c) is normalized to $h_{k-n}/\max h$ (0–1), and (d) in raw $h_k^{(i)}$ (−0.12–0.2). Four different y-scalings, one of them normalized, invites the reader to mis-read relative taper energy. The (e)–(h) row, by contrast, is correctly placed on a shared 0–0.20 scale, which is the right choice and should be extended in spirit to the top row (or the normalization difference made explicit in-panel, not only in the caption).

AXIS TRUNCATION:    None — All of (e)–(h) share an identical $y$ range beginning at 0.00, so bar heights are proportional to value; no exaggeration. Top-row y-ranges differ but are not truncated in a way that inflates a difference within a panel.

OVERPLOTTING:       Present — Two distinct problems.
(i) Panel (a): all seven time-shifted Hann segments are drawn in a *single* colour with no lightness gradient (unlike b–d, where the caption promises lighter shades for later members). Individual members are indistinguishable and the 50% overlap structure must be inferred. Where two adjacent segments cross, the identity of each lobe is lost.
(ii) Panel (c): the four sinc positions are heavily superimposed in the near-zero sidelobe region, which collapses into an unreadable band of overlapping oscillations across most of $0<k<256$. Only the main lobes are legible.

MULTIPLE COMPARISONS: No concern — No inferential comparisons are annotated; the four $\nu$ values are deterministic functionals of the ladders, not test statistics.

TIME SERIES:        Concern — $h_k$ is defined only on the integer sample grid $k=0,\dots,255$, but is rendered as a continuous interpolated curve. For (a), (b) and (d) the sequences are smooth over many samples and the interpolation is harmless. For (c), however, the sinc window's sidelobe oscillation has a period of only a few samples; drawing it as a continuous line (and at this figure width, with likely line-decimation on rendering) risks displaying a *rendering artefact* rather than the true sample values — apparent sidelobe amplitudes and zero crossings may not correspond to actual $h_k$. This is the classic misleading-interpolation-across-a-coarse-grid problem, here in the sample domain rather than the time domain. Intervals are even, so no gap-interpolation issue.

ISSUES:
  - [BLOCKING] Panel (c) plots a rapidly oscillating discrete sequence as a continuous line at a resolution where the oscillation period approaches the pixel/sample scale. The displayed sidelobe structure cannot be verified as the true sampled sequence. Authors must confirm no decimation/aliasing in rendering and demonstrate sample-level fidelity.
  - [BLOCKING] Panel (a) does not implement the lightness gradient that the caption asserts for the bank members ("lighter shades for later members of the bank in (b–d)" excludes (a), but (a) is the only panel showing *all* seven members and is therefore the one most in need of member discrimination). As drawn, the seven segments are not individually identifiable.
  - [ADVISORY] The number of bars (bank size) in (e)–(h) is not annotated in-panel, and x-ticks at 5 and 10 only do not let the reader read off the count or the index of the last non-negligible weight.
  - [ADVISORY] Four different y-axis scalings across (a)–(d), including a max-normalization in (c) alone, prevent valid visual comparison of taper amplitudes across routes; the normalization is disclosed only in the axis label, which is easy to miss.
  - [ADVISORY] (a)–(d) show a subset of each bank (seven of seven in (a); four of a continuum in (b); four positions in (c); four of seven in (d)), but which members are shown is not marked in-panel; legends in (b)–(d) give the selector ($f'N\Delta$, $i$) while (a) gives none.
  - [ADVISORY] In (f) and (g) the ladder decays smoothly to near zero, so the plotted bar count is a display cutoff rather than an intrinsic bank size. The cutoff rule (e.g. $c_i/\sum c_i$ below some threshold) is not stated, yet it determines the apparent width of the ladder that the reader associates with $\nu$.
  - [ADVISORY] $\nu$ is printed in each of (e)–(h) but the flatness it summarizes is a property of the whole ladder including the suppressed tail; readers cannot check $\nu=2(\sum c_i)^2/\sum c_i^2$ against the bars if tail bars are cut off or sub-pixel.

SUGGESTED FIXES:
  - Apply the same light-to-dark shade ramp used in (b)–(d) to the seven segments in (a), or stagger them into a small vertical offset / ridgeline so each member is separable.
  - For (c), either (i) plot markers at the actual sample positions (or a stem/step rendering) over a restricted $k$ window in an inset, or (ii) show $|h_k|$ on a log y-axis so the sidelobes are resolved rather than collapsed against zero. Confirm the exported vector path contains all 256 points.
  - Put the bank size in each of (e)–(h) next to $\nu$ (e.g. "$L=7$, $\nu=13.4$"), and add minor x-ticks at every integer $i$ so bar counts are readable.
  - Either place (a)–(d) on a common y-scale in raw window units, or normalize *all four* identically and say so in the axis label of every panel; avoid normalizing one panel only.
  - Mark in-panel which members are plotted in (b) and (c) (they already have legends) and add an equivalent legend or annotation to (a); state in the caption that (a) shows the complete bank while (b)–(d) show subsets.
  - State the display cutoff for (f) and (g) in the caption, and consider a log y-axis or an inset for the tail so that the ladder supporting $\nu=16.7$ and $17.2$ is fully visible.

---

### figure6.png

---
PLOT TYPE:          Appropriate — These are line plots of continuous functions (estimated PSD vs. frequency; smoothing kernels vs. frequency). This is the correct encoding; none of the bar/pie/error-bar concerns apply. Overlaying the true PSD in black in every panel and repeating the same kernel-inset design across (e–h) is good practice for direct visual comparison. The log (dB) ordinate is appropriate for spectra spanning ~60–150 dB.

SAMPLE SIZE:        Not visible — The figure itself gives no indication that only **one realization** is plotted; this appears only in the caption ("one realization"). The record length ($N=1024$) and the ensemble size (300 realizations) used for the quoted summary errors are also caption-only. Partial credit: the annotated equivalent degrees of freedom ($\nu=17.2, 14.0, 16.7, 17.6$) do function as an in-figure statement of the effective sample size behind each estimate, which is the right instinct — extend it by adding "1 realization, $N=1024$" as an in-panel annotation.

COMPARISON:         Concern — Two distinct issues:
  1. **Representativeness.** The caption concedes "This realization has more leakage than is typical," and the numbers confirm it: median absolute errors for this draw are 11.6 / 4.6 / 0.9 / 0.8 dB versus ensemble values of 5.3 / 2.3 / 1.0 / 1.0 dB. For methods (a) and (b) the displayed realization is roughly 2× worse than typical, while for (c) and (d) it is essentially typical. The *visual* contrast between the left and right panels therefore exaggerates the ensemble-average performance gap, and nothing in the graphic signals this. This is the statistical-visualization analogue of showing an unrepresentative exemplar.
  2. **Matched-bandwidth caveat.** The four kernels are matched only "about" in half-power width (8.2, 7.2, 8.2, 7.8 $/(N\Delta)$), a ~14% spread, and $\nu$ ranges 14.0–17.6 (~25%). Method (b) is thus given the least smoothing and will necessarily look noisiest. This is disclosed in the caption but not visible in the figure.
  3. This is paired/common-input data (all four estimators applied to the *same* record), which is the strong design — but the panel layout does not convey the pairing. Since the four estimates share an abscissa and a reference curve, they could be over-plotted in one panel (or differenced against the true PSD) to make the paired structure explicit.

AXIS TRUNCATION:    Present — Not misleading in the main panels: (a–d) span −40 to 60 dB with data confined to roughly −22 to 45 dB, so no compression or exaggeration; (e–h) span −150 to 0 dB and the curves reach the lower limit, but the range is generous and identical across panels. Two items to check:
  - The (e–h) insets are clipped at −50 dB, which truncates the Fejér sidelobe structure in (e) and the Slepian ripple floor in (f) exactly where the leakage argument is being made. The inset is doing rhetorical work with a range that cuts off part of the relevant evidence.
  - In (c) and (d) the coloured estimate appears to terminate slightly short of $f\Delta=0.5$ (visibly so in (d), ~0.49), and in (g) the kernel reaches the frame edge at −100 dB. Confirm these are genuine domain limits (Welch segment/Nyquist bookkeeping) and not truncated rendering; an unexplained stop at the right edge invites the reader to assume the curve continues favourably.
  - All panels share identical axis ranges across the row — good, and essential here.

OVERPLOTTING:       Present — Panel (b) is the main case: the multitaper estimate above $f\Delta\approx0.25$ is a dense, high-frequency oscillation drawn at full opacity and full line weight, so the trace saturates into a solid band and the local density/envelope of the fluctuation is unreadable. The same occurs in the (f) inset ripple and, mildly, in the (h) kernel sidelobes. Because panel (b) is precisely where the "levels off 10 dB above the true spectrum" claim is made, the saturated band obscures whether the reader is seeing a bias offset or a variance envelope.

MULTIPLE COMPARISONS: No concern — No inferential annotations, significance markers, or pairwise tests appear in the figure, so no correction issue arises. One related caution: four estimators are compared on a single shared realization and summary error statistics are quoted in the caption for both that realization and a 300-member ensemble; if any claim of superiority is intended to be statistical, the ensemble distributions (not point medians) should support it.

TIME SERIES:        Not applicable — These are frequency-domain functions on a complete, evenly sampled $f\Delta \in [0, 0.5]$ grid; there are no temporal gaps or interpolation across missing timepoints. (Verify only the right-edge terminations noted above.)

ISSUES:
  - [BLOCKING] The plotted realization is explicitly atypical (per-realization median absolute errors 11.6 and 4.6 dB for methods (a) and (b), versus ensemble values of 5.3 and 2.3 dB), so the figure visually overstates the performance gap between the left and right panels. The graphic carries no in-figure indication of this, and readers who do not parse the caption sentence "This realization has more leakage than is typical" will take the displayed contrast as representative.
  - [BLOCKING] $n$ is not visible or inferable from the figure: nothing in the panels indicates that a single realization is shown. With $n=1$, the reader cannot distinguish reproducible bias from one draw of the sampling variability — most critically in (a) and (b), where the "levels off 20 dB / 10 dB above the true spectrum" claims are bias claims being supported by a single trace.
  - [ADVISORY] Kernel bandwidths and degrees of freedom are only approximately matched (half-power widths 7.2–8.2 $/(N\Delta)$; $\nu$ 14.0–17.6). Method (b) receives the least smoothing and is therefore penalized visually. The matching tolerance is caption-only.
  - [ADVISORY] Overplotting/saturation of the dense multitaper fluctuation in (b) (and in the (f) inset) prevents the reader from judging the fluctuation envelope, which is the quantity at issue.
  - [ADVISORY] Inset ordinate clipped at −50 dB in (e–h) truncates the sidelobe/ripple structure central to the leakage argument.
  - [ADVISORY] The paired design (all four estimators on one common record) is not visually expressed; the four-panel layout reads as four independent experiments.
  - [ADVISORY] The legend appears only as coloured text in (a) ("True PSD" / "Estimate"), with colour as the sole channel distinguishing estimate from truth in (b–d). Check greyscale and colour-vision-deficiency legibility of the green/blue/orange/pink set against black.
  - [ADVISORY] Apparent short termination of the estimate traces before $f\Delta=0.5$ in (c) and especially (d) is unexplained.

SUGGESTED FIXES:
  - Add an in-figure uncertainty band: overlay the pointwise ensemble median and, say, 10th–90th percentile envelope from the existing 300 realizations behind each single-realization trace. This converts $n=1$ anecdote into a distributional statement at no cost, since the ensemble is already computed, and directly resolves the bias-versus-variance ambiguity in (a) and (b).
  - Annotate each of (a–d) in-panel with "1 realization, $N=1024$" and the per-realization median absolute error alongside the existing $\nu$, and mark the atypical panels (e.g., "this draw: 11.6 dB; ensemble median: 5.3 dB"). The reader should not need the caption to learn that the exemplar is unrepresentative.
  - Alternatively or additionally, replace the exemplar with a *typical* realization (one whose errors sit near the ensemble medians) and move the high-leakage draw to a supplement, or show both side by side. If the atypical draw is retained deliberately to illustrate the leakage failure mode, state that purpose in the panel itself.
  - Add a fifth panel plotting $\hat S(f) - S(f)$ (error in dB) for all four methods on common axes. This makes the paired structure explicit, removes the eye's need to compare four separate black reference curves, and shows the bias floors in (a) and (b) as flat offsets rather than as visually inferred asymptotes.
  - Reduce line weight and apply ~40–60% opacity to the dense estimate traces (especially (b)); optionally add a heavier smoothed median line through the fluctuation so the envelope and its centre are both readable.
  - Extend the (e–h) inset ordinate to at least −60 to −70 dB, or add a second inset at the sidelobe scale, so the Fejér tails and Slepian ripple floor are fully visible rather than clipped.
  - Report the bandwidth-matching tolerance in the figure (e.g., a small table strip giving half-power width and $\nu$ per panel), so the reader can see that (b) is the least-smoothed case when judging its noisiness.
  - Confirm and explain the right-edge termination of the (c)/(d) traces; if the estimator is undefined near Nyquist, mark the excluded interval with shading rather than letting the curve simply stop.
  - Verify colour choices with a CVD simulator and add a redundant channel (dash pattern or direct in-panel text label next to each trace) so the estimate/truth distinction survives greyscale reproduction.

### figure7.png

---

**Context note:** This is a signal-processing comparison figure (spectral density estimates of a single EEG epoch versus frequency), not a group-comparison or replicate-based figure. Several checklist items (bar/pie plotting, pairwise significance annotation, subject-level dot plots) are therefore inapplicable, and I have marked them as such rather than forcing a criticism. The items that *do* apply are dynamic-range compression, visibility of the uncertainty that underwrites the caption's central claim, and overplotting of three near-coincident traces.

**PLOT TYPE:** Appropriate — Overlaid line traces are the correct choice for continuous spectral estimates on a dense frequency grid. The two-panel design (absolute estimates above, differences below) is exactly the right construction: panel (a) alone would be unreadable for the 0.6–0.9 dB discrepancies at issue, and panel (b) supplies the residual view. No bar/pie/error-bar-summary concerns arise. One structural note: the quantity the caption claims ("agree to within their own noise") is a *comparison of residuals to a noise scale*, and that noise scale is absent from the plotting surface.

**SAMPLE SIZE:** Not visible — The effective sample size here is the degrees of freedom of each estimator ($\nu=14$, $16.7$, $17.6$) plus the fact that this is a single EEG epoch (n = 1 record). None of this is inferable from the figure; all of it lives in the caption. For a figure whose thesis is a noise-level comparison, $\nu$ should appear in the legend entries (e.g. "Multitaper, $L=7$ ($\nu=14$)"), since $\nu$ is what sets the noise floor the reader is being asked to judge against. Individual "data points" (raw periodogram ordinates) are deliberately and correctly not shown — they would be $\chi^2_2$ noise and would obscure everything.

**COMPARISON:** Concern (minor, remediable) — The three estimates are computed from the *same* epoch, so these are paired/matched curves, and panel (b) correctly presents them in paired form as differences from a common reference. That is good practice. Two residual issues: (i) the choice of the multitaper estimate as the zero reference is arbitrary and is not flagged as such in the panel — a reader may infer the multitaper result is ground truth, when in fact the caption reveals it is the trace with the *worst* valley behaviour (seventh Slepian side lobes filling in the nulls); (ii) the half-power widths differ (0.91, 1.03, 0.97 Hz), so part of the plotted difference is a resolution mismatch rather than estimator noise. Neither is visible in the figure.

**AXIS TRUNCATION:** Present — not misleading. Panel (a) spans roughly 7–48 dB with no zero baseline. On a decibel (logarithmic) axis a zero origin is meaningless, and no bars are involved, so this is standard and acceptable. Note the *direction* of the distortion is conservative, not inflationary: the ~40 dB dynamic range in (a) visually compresses the inter-estimator disagreement and makes the curves look more coincident than they are. Panel (b), plotted on ±15 dB while the data occupy roughly −5 to +12.5 dB, likewise understates rather than exaggerates. Panel (a) carries no x-tick labels; this is acceptable for a shared-axis stacked layout, but the two panels must be verified to share identical x-limits (they appear to, 0–40 Hz).

**OVERPLOTTING:** Present — This is the main visual defect. In panel (a) three fully opaque traces occupy nearly the same path. The blue multitaper curve appears to be drawn first and is occluded by the orange and pink curves over most of the range; it is visible mainly where it departs from them (the valleys and the ~0–1 Hz region). A reader cannot tell whether blue is hidden or absent in the agreeing regions, which undercuts the "agree to within their own noise" claim precisely where agreement is best. In panel (b) the orange and pink residual traces cross repeatedly and the orange is frequently buried under the pink at the peaks.

**MULTIPLE COMPARISONS:** No concern — No hypothesis tests or pairwise significance annotations are present. The caption reports descriptive summaries (medians, 95th percentiles) rather than p-values, so no correction issue arises. Do note that the 95th percentile of a residual evaluated over a dense frequency grid with strongly correlated neighbouring ordinates is not an independent-sample quantile; if that number is used to support any inferential claim, the effective number of independent frequency bins ($\approx$ bandwidth / Rayleigh spacing) should be stated.

**TIME SERIES:** Not applicable — The abscissa is frequency on a uniform grid with no gaps, so there is no interpolation-across-missing-data risk. (The underlying epoch length and sampling rate that set the grid spacing are not recoverable from the figure; see fixes.)

**ISSUES:**
- [ADVISORY] The noise scale that the entire claim rests on (±1.6, ±1.5, ±1.5 dB, from $4.3\sqrt{2/\nu}$) is stated only in prose. The reader cannot perform the comparison the caption asserts by looking at panel (b).
- [ADVISORY] Opaque overplotting in panel (a) hides the blue multitaper trace wherever the three estimators agree, i.e. over most of the plotted range.
- [ADVISORY] Degrees of freedom $\nu$ and half-power widths — the parameters that determine both the noise floor and the resolution mismatch — are absent from the plotting surface.
- [ADVISORY] The multitaper estimate serves as the unmarked zero reference in (b), inviting a false reading of it as ground truth despite the caption identifying its side-lobe leakage as the source of the largest deviations.
- [ADVISORY] Differing half-power widths (0.91 vs 1.03 vs 0.97 Hz) mean panel (b) mixes resolution bias with estimator variance; the two contributions are not separated.
- [ADVISORY] The ±15 dB range in (b) leaves roughly a third of the panel empty, reducing the visual resolution of the residuals that are the point of the panel.

**SUGGESTED FIXES:**
- Add a shaded horizontal band at ±1.6 dB (or ±1.5 dB, whichever is defended as the relevant single-estimator noise level, or ±$\sqrt{2}\times$ that for a difference of two independent estimates) across panel (b), labelled "estimator noise, 1 s.d." This converts the caption's claim into something the reader can verify at a glance and is the single highest-value change to the figure.
- Reduce panel (b) y-limits to approximately −6 to +13 dB to use the available area, or retain symmetry at ±13 dB.
- Fix the draw order and transparency in panel (a): plot the multitaper reference last, or as a thicker semi-transparent grey/blue band with the two comparison estimates as thinner opaque lines on top. Alternatively add a small inset magnifying one representative harmonic peak-and-valley pair (e.g. 8.5–9.5 Hz, where panel (b) shows the largest excursion) so the reader can see the three traces resolved.
- Append $\nu$ to each legend entry in (a), and add the half-power width, e.g. "Multitaper, $L=7$ ($\nu=14$, $R_{1/2}=0.91$ Hz)."
- Label the zero line in (b) explicitly as "multitaper reference" and state in the caption that the reference is a choice of baseline, not a standard, given that its side lobes are identified as the dominant source of disagreement.
- State the epoch length, sampling rate, and frequency-grid spacing in the caption so the effective number of independent frequency bins behind the median and 95th-percentile statistics is recoverable.
- Consider a third panel, or a colour/marker overlay in (b), distinguishing valley frequencies from peak frequencies, since the caption's mechanistic explanation (side-lobe fill-in at spectral nulls) is currently asserted rather than shown.
- Confirm the two panels share identical x-limits and that panel (a)'s suppressed tick labels correspond exactly to panel (b)'s.

### figure8.png

---
**PLOT TYPE:** Appropriate (with reservations) — Time trace, spectrograms and spectral overlays are the correct idioms for this material; none of the bar/pie/error-bar pathologies apply. Two reservations: (i) panels (c,e,g) use a rainbow (jet-like) colour map, which is perceptually non-uniform and creates false contours/edges at the yellow–cyan and red transitions — precisely the visual features a reader will interpret as spectral structure, and it is not colour-vision-safe; a sequential perceptually uniform map (viridis, magma, cividis) is the defensible choice for a methods-comparison figure whose whole point is that one estimate is smoother than another. (ii) Panel (h) is a marginal histogram of a quantity that is strongly structured in time and frequency; it is appropriate as a summary but should be supplemented (see fixes).

**SAMPLE SIZE:** Not visible — This is an n = 1 demonstration: one subject, one seizure, one channel, and the figure itself carries no statement to that effect. The caption gives the window count (599 windows, 2 s stepped by 1 s) but not the number of pixels entering (h), not the number of recordings, and not the number of channels screened. For a figure that is implicitly making a performance claim about three estimators, the reader cannot tell from the figure whether this is an illustrative single case or a representative case.
